9.2.1  How to do it with R

We can do Example 9.13 with the following code.

> with(PlantGrowth, z.test(weight, sd = 0.7))

        One Sample z-test

data:  weight
z = 39.694, n = 30.0000, Std. Dev. = 0.7000,
Std. Dev. of the sample mean = 0.1278, p-value <
2.2e-16
alternative hypothesis: true mean is not equal to 0
95 percent confidence interval:
 4.822513 5.323487
sample estimates:
mean of weight
         5.073

   The conﬁdence interval bounds are shown in the sixth line down of the output (please disregard all of the
additional output information for now – we will use it in Chapter 10).
  We can do Example 9.16 with the following code.

> t.test(x, conf.level = 0.99)

        One Sample t-test

data:  x
t = 38.743, df = 19, p-value < 2.2e-16
alternative hypothesis: true mean is not equal to 0
99 percent confidence interval:
 841.8762 976.1238
sample estimates:
mean of x
      909

  We see the conﬁdence interval bounds shown in the ﬁfth line of the output. If we had wanted to compute
a 99% one-sided interval instead, we can could have achieved it with the alternative argument to t.test
in the following way:

> t.test(x, conf.level = 0.99, alternative = "greater")

        One Sample t-test

data:  x
t = 38.743, df = 19, p-value < 2.2e-16
alternative hypothesis: true mean is greater than 0
99 percent confidence interval:
 849.4182      Inf
sample estimates:
mean of x
      909

The command with alternative = "greater" yields an interval with a lower bound for µ, in this case
[841, ∞), while alternative = "less" gives the interval with an upper bound for µ (easy to remember,
because it is opposite from what a person would expect it to be).



186                                              CHAPTER 9. ESTIMATION

9.3  Conﬁdence Intervals for Diﬀerences of Means

Let X1, X2, . . . , Xn be a S RS (n) from a norm(mean = µX, sd = σX) distribution and let Y1, Y2, ..., Ym be a
S RS (m) from a norm(mean = µY, sd = σY) distribution. Further, assume that the X1, X2, ..., Xn sample is
independent of the Y1, Y2, ..., Ym sample.
   Suppose that σX and σY are known. We would like a conﬁdence interval for µX −µY. We know that

                              s
                                                      σ2X   σ2Y
                  X −Y ∼norm     = µX −µY, sd =     +                           (9.16)
                                                        n   m                                                                                               mean                                                                                                                                                                    .

Therefore, a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for µX −µY is given by

                         s
                                             σ2X   σ2Y
                           X −Y ± zα/2    +       .                                  (9.17)
                                               n   m

Unfortunately, most of the time the values of σX and σY are unknown. This leads us to the following:

   •  If both sample sizes are large, then we may appeal to the CLT/SLLN (see Section 8.3) and substitute S 2X
     and S Y2 for σ2X and σ2Y in the interval (9.17). The resulting conﬁdence interval will have approximately
     100(1 −α)% conﬁdence.

   •  If one or more of the sample sizes is small then we are in trouble, unless the underlying populations
      are both normal and σX = σY. In this case (setting σ = σX = σY),

                               r 1   1
                    X −Y ∼norm     = µX −µY, sd = σ   +                        (9.18)                                                                                 mean                  n  m .

    Now let
                                        n −1  2  m −1  2
                        U =     S X +      S Y.                                (9.19)                                       σ2       σ2
     Then by Exercise 28 we know that U ∼chisq(df = n + m −2) and it is not a large leap to believe that
   U is independent of X −Y; thus

                                   Z
                          T = √U/ (n + m −2) ∼t(df = n + m −2).                       (9.20)

     But



                                                                                                   1                                                         X−Y−(µX−µY)σ√ 1n + m
                            T =                                                    ,
                     q n−1                                                    σ2 S 2X + m−1σ2 S Y2 . (n + m −2)

                                X −Y −(µX −µY)
                          =                                              ,
                   r                                                      1    1    (n−1)S 2X+(m−1)S Y2                               +                                                      n   m       n+m−2

                                ∼t(df = n + m −2).

      Therefore a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for µX −µY is given by

                              r 1   1
                        X −Y ± tα/2(df = n + m −2) S p   +                          (9.21)                                                          n  m,

     where
                     s          2              2
                                                (n −1)S X + (m −1)S Y
                                 S p =                                                        (9.22)
                                             n + m −2
       is called the “pooled” estimator of σ.



9.3. CONFIDENCE INTERVALS FOR DIFFERENCES OF MEANS                           187

   •  If one of the samples is small, and both underlying populations are normal, but σX , σY, then we may
      use a Welch (or Satterthwaite) approximation to the degrees of freedom. See Welch [216], Satterthwaite
      [184], or Neter et al [146]. The idea is to use an interval of the form

                            s  2      2
                                                    S X   S Y
                           X −Y ± tα/2(df = r)     +      ,                           (9.23)
                                                     n   m

     where the degrees of freedom r is chosen so that the interval has nice statistical properties. It turns out
       that a good choice for r is given by

                                                                            2
                                         S 2X/n + S Y/m2
                                          r =                                              ,                            (9.24)                                                                2                 2
                                                n−1                                                     1  S 2X/n  + m−11  S Y/m2

     The resulting interval has approximately 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence.

Example 9.17 (Tooth Growth in Guinea Pigs). In the ToothGrowth data, the response variable len is the
length of odontoblasts (cells responsible for tooth growth) measured on 60 guinea pigs. Each animal received
vitamin C by one of two delivery methods recorded in the variable supp, taking values "OJ" for orange juice
or "VC" for ascorbic acid (a form of vitamin C). We would like a 99% two-sided conﬁdence interval for the
diﬀerence in means, µVC −µOJ, where µVC is the true mean odontoblast length for the VC group and µOJ is
the true mean odontoblast length for the OJ group.

9.3.1  How to do it with R

The basic function is t.test which has a var.equal argument that may be set to TRUE or FALSE, where the
default is FALSE. The conﬁdence interval is shown as part of the output, although there is a lot of additional
information that is not needed until Chapter 10.
   There is not any speciﬁc functionality to handle the z-interval for small samples, but if the samples are
large then t.test with var.equal = FALSE will be essentially the same thing. The standard deviations are
never (?) known in advance anyway so it does not really matter in practice.
   To make it more concrete, suppose we would like to tackle Example 9.17. First we will need the data.
Let us separate the len data into a VC vector x and an OJ vector y:

> x = with(ToothGrowth, len[supp == "VC"])
> y = with(ToothGrowth, len[supp == "OJ"])

  Now it turns out that both x and y are exactly length 30, so it would be fair play to declare “large sample!”
and go on about our business. But we are not going to do that. Let us check normality of the data.
   Judging from Figure 9.6 both groups are normally distributed. In the absence of additional information
we may move forward with a Welch-Aspin interval, thusly:

> t.test(x, y, conf.level = 0.99)

        Welch Two Sample t-test

data:  x and y
t = -1.9153, df = 55.309, p-value = 0.06063
alternative hypothesis: true difference in means is not equal to 0
99 percent confidence interval:
 -8.853546  1.453546
sample estimates:
mean of x mean of y
 16.96333  20.66333



188                                              CHAPTER 9. ESTIMATION





        35                                       23                                               26
        25

  x
        15


    5

               −2       −1       0        1        2

                            norm quantiles





        30


  y 20

        10        8                7

               −2       −1       0        1        2

                            norm quantiles




Figure 9.6: These are graphs of normal quantile-quantile comparison plots of the VC supplement group and the OJ
supplement group in the ToothGrowth data. These data appear normal. The graphs were generated by the qqPlot
function from the RcmdrMisc package.



9.4. CONFIDENCE INTERVALS FOR PROPORTIONS                                     189


     If we had reason to believe that the variances in the two groups were equal we could instead use the
Pooled t interval like this:

> t.test(x, y, conf.level = 0.99, var.equal = TRUE)

        Two Sample t-test

data:  x and y
t = -1.9153, df = 58, p-value = 0.06039
alternative hypothesis: true difference in means is not equal to 0
99 percent confidence interval:
 -8.845056  1.445056
sample estimates:
mean of x mean of y
 16.96333  20.66333

  We can skip all of the subsetting into x and y vectors by using the formula interface to t.test. The gen-
eral form is t.test(response   groups, data = A), where A is a data frame containing the response
variable and a groups variable. Here is how the Welch interval looks for ToothGrowth:

> t.test(len ~ supp, data = ToothGrowth, conf.level = 0.99)

        Welch Two Sample t-test

data:  len by supp
t = 1.9153, df = 55.309, p-value = 0.06063
alternative hypothesis: true difference in means between group OJ and group VC is not equa
99 percent confidence interval:
 -1.453546  8.853546
sample estimates:
mean in group OJ mean in group VC
        20.66333         16.96333

   Watch out! Notice with the formula interface that R is now computing a 99% conﬁdence interval for
µOJ−µVC, and not µVC−µOJ, as before. Why? The computer takes the levels of the factor supp in alphabetical
order by default, and "OJ" comes before "VC".
    In closing, there are three things to notice about this example:

   1. Regardless of whether we choose Welch or Pooled, the 99% conﬁdence interval covers the value zero.
      In other words, with 99% conﬁdence, it is plausible that the means are the same (µVC −µOJ = 0).

   2. Notice that the Pooled interval was ever-so-slightly more narrow than the Welch interval. This is not
       entirely a surprise; we would expect additional information such as the population variances are equal
      to generally increase the precision of our interval estimate, and in fact this is what we observed in this
      example.

   3. We can save ourselves some typing by way of the formula interface to t.test, among other functions.
     But PAY CLOSE ATTENTION to what parameter is being estimated and what the computer is doing
     under the hood.

9.4  Conﬁdence Intervals for Proportions

In this section we are trying to estimate p which is the “proportion of successes”. For instance, p could be:

   • the proportion of U.S. citizens that support Obama,



190                                              CHAPTER 9. ESTIMATION


   • the proportion of smokers among adults age 18 or over,

   • the proportion of people worldwide aﬄicted by COVID-19.

  We are given an S RS (n) X1, X2, ..., Xn distributed binom(size = 1, prob = p). Recall from Section 5.3
that the common mean of these variables is EX = p and the variance is E(X −p)2 = p(1 −p).  If we let
Y = P Xi, then from Section 5.3 we know that Y ∼binom(size = n, prob = p) and that

                           Y                         p(1 −p)
                     X =   has EX = p and Var(X) =                .
                              n                         n

Thus if n is large (here is the CLT) then an approximate 100(1−α)% conﬁdence interval for p would be given
by
                      r p(1 −p)
                             X ± zα/2                   .                                      (9.25)
                                               n

OOPS.. . ! Equation (9.25) is of no use to us because the unknown parameter p is in the formula! (If we knew
what p was to plug in the formula then we would not need a conﬁdence interval in the ﬁrst place.) There are
two solutions to this problem.

   1. Replace p with ˆp = X. Then an approximate 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for p is given by

                       r ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                                                                            ˆp ± zα/2                   .                                   (9.26)
                                                 n

      This approach is called the Wald interval and is also known as the asymptotic interval because it appeals
      to the CLT for large sample sizes.

   2. Go back to ﬁrst principles. Note that

                                            Y/n −p
                                         −zα/2 ≤             ≤zα/2
                            p p(1 −p)/n

      exactly when the function f deﬁned by

                                                           p(1 −p)
                                       f(p) = (Y/n −p)2 −z2α/2                                                       n

      satisﬁes f(p) ≤0. But  f  is quadratic in p so its graph is a parabola;  it has two roots, and these
      roots form the limits of the conﬁdence interval. We can ﬁnd them with the quadratic formula (see
      Exercise 35):
                      s
                                          z2α/2             ˆp(1 −ˆp)    z2α/2  ,      z2α/2
                       +         zα/2        +       +                         (9.27)                                                                                                                          ˆp   2n  ±         n       (2n)2          1   n                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         

      This approach is called the score interval because it is based on the inversion of the “Score test”. See
      Chapter 15. It is also known as the Wilson interval; see Agresti [2].

   For two proportions p1 and p2, we may collect independent binom(size = 1, prob = p) samples of size
n1 and n2, respectively. Let Y1 and Y2 denote the number of successes in the respective samples. We know
that
                            Y1            r p1(1 −p1)
                       ≈norm     = p1, sd =                              n1               mean                   n1                           

and
                            Y2            r p2(1 −p2)
                       ≈norm     = p2, sd =                              n2               mean                   n2                           



9.4. CONFIDENCE INTERVALS FOR PROPORTIONS                                     191


so it stands to reason that an approximate 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for p1 −p2 is given by

                     r ˆp1(1 −ˆp1)    ˆp2(1 −ˆp2)
                                       ( ˆp1 −ˆp2) ± zα/2         +                   ,                          (9.28)
                                                    n1           n2

where ˆp1 = Y1/n1 and ˆp2 = Y2/n2.
Remark 9.18. When estimating a single proportion, one-sided intervals are sometimes needed. They take the
form
                       r ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                                                                           ˆp + zα                                                 (9.29)                                                                                                                                           0,           n                              
or
                     r ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                                 −zα                   , 1                                     (9.30)                                                                                                                                                                     ˆp         n                                                     

or in other words, we know in advance that the true proportion is restricted to the interval [0, 1], so we can
truncate our conﬁdence interval to those values on either side.

9.4.1  How to do it with R

Conﬁdence interval estimation (two-sided only) of a single population proportion is perhaps slickest done
through the binconf function in the Hmisc package. For the Wald interval set method = "asymptotic".
Below x represents the number of successes in the sample, n represents the sample size, and alpha represents
1 minus the conﬁdence level. So, for a 95% conﬁdence interval for p after we observed 7 successes in 25
trials, we would do

> library(Hmisc)
> binconf(x = 7, n = 25, alpha = 0.05, method = "asymptotic")

 PointEst     Lower     Upper
     0.28 0.1039957 0.4560043

   For the score interval set method = "wilson":

> binconf(x = 7, n = 25, alpha = 0.05, method = "wilson")

 PointEst     Lower     Upper
     0.28 0.1428385 0.4757661

   The default value of the alpha argument is 0.05 and the default method argument is wilson:

> binconf(x = 7, n = 25)

 PointEst     Lower     Upper
     0.28 0.1428385 0.4757661

   Another way to get the same Wilson interval is to use the prop.test function, but there we must specify
the conﬁdence level directly, and be sure to turn oﬀthe Yates’ continuity correction:

> prop.test(x = 7, n = 25, conf.level = 0.95, correct = FALSE)

        1-sample proportions test without continuity
        correction

data:  7 out of 25, null probability 0.5
X-squared = 4.84, df = 1, p-value = 0.02781



192                                              CHAPTER 9. ESTIMATION


alternative hypothesis: true p is not equal to 0.5
95 percent confidence interval:
 0.1428385 0.4757661
sample estimates:
   p
0.28

   The binconf function does not support one-sided conﬁdence intervals, but prop.test does; request
them via the alternative argument. Continuing this example, to get a 95% one-sided conﬁdence interval
for p that provides a lower bound do

> prop.test(x = 7, n = 25, alternative = "greater", correct = FALSE)

        1-sample proportions test without continuity
        correction

data:  7 out of 25, null probability 0.5
X-squared = 4.84, df = 1, p-value = 0.9861
alternative hypothesis: true p is greater than 0.5
95 percent confidence interval:
 0.1595384 1.0000000
sample estimates:
   p
0.28

   Note that the default value of conf.level in prop.test is 0.95, so we skipped that argument in this
example. A one-sided 95% interval that gives an upper bound is found with alternative = "less", that
is, the opposite of what a normal human would expect it to be.
    In the two-sample problem we are constructing a conﬁdence interval for the diﬀerence, p1 −p2. In this
case we are limited to the prop.test function. The syntax is the same, except our numbers of successes
are now a vector x = c(y1, y2) and the sample sizes are a vector n = c(n1, n2). For instance, if we
observed 7 successes out of 25 trials in the ﬁrst sample and 11 successes out of 37 trials in the second
(independent) sample, a 99% two-sided conﬁdence interval for p1 −p2 would be given by

> prop.test(x = c(7,11), n = c(25,37),
+           conf.level = 0.99, correct = FALSE)

        2-sample test for equality of proportions
        without continuity correction

data:  c(7, 11) out of c(25, 37)
X-squared = 0.021665, df = 1, p-value = 0.883
alternative hypothesis: two.sided
99 percent confidence interval:
 -0.3189033  0.2843087
sample estimates:
   prop 1    prop 2
0.2800000 0.2972973

9.5  Conﬁdence Intervals for Variances

We are given X1, X2, ..., Xn that are an S RS (n) from a norm(mean = µ, sd = σ) distribution, where this
time σ is unknown and the target of our interest. We know that we may estimate σ2 with S 2, and we have



9.5. CONFIDENCE INTERVALS FOR VARIANCES                                        193


seen that this estimator is unbiased. But how good is our estimate? We know that

                                     (n −1)S 2
                                       ∼chisq(df = n −1).                                (9.31)
                                 σ2

For a big probability 1 −α we can calculate the quantile χ2α/2(n −1), but the Chi-square distribution is
not symmetric about zero, so this time we will need a second quantile for the left-hand inequality, namely,
χ21−α/2(n −1). Then
                                    −1)S 2               !                    P χ21−α/2(n −1) ≤(n      ≤χ2α/2(n −1) = 1 −α.                      (9.32)                                       σ2

Next, solving this string of inequalities to put σ2 in the middle leads to:

                                 (n −1)S 2           (n −1)S 2
                      P         ≤σ2 ≤               1 −α.                         (9.33)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     χ2α/2(n −1)                                                χ21−α/2(n −1) =

That is, a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for σ2 is given by

                                        (n −1)S 2     (n −1)S 2
                                                                                                      (9.34)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  χ2α/2(n −1),                                                χ21−α/2(n −1) .

    In the two-sample problem, we are given X1, X2, ..., Xn a S RS (n) from a norm(mean = µX, sd = σX)
distribution and Y1, Y2, ..., Ym a S RS (m) from a norm(mean = µY, sd = σY) distribution that is independent
of the ﬁrst sample. From Section 8.4.3 we know that

                             σ2YS 2X                     F =      ∼f(df1 = n −1, df2 = m −1).
                             σ2XS Y2

Next ﬁnd the quantiles f1−α/2(n −1, m −1) and fα/2(n −1, m −1). Then


                                                  X                           P    ≤σ2YS 2                                                             2 ≤fα/2   1 −α,                               (9.35)                                                                          f1−α/2   σ2XS Y               =

or, in other words,
                                     S 2X/S Y2  S 2X/S Y2
                                                                                      ,                                                (9.36)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            f1−α/2      fα/2              
is a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for σ2X/σ2Y.
Remark 9.19. In this section we have derived two conﬁdence intervals, and how they work out is really quite
beautiful as far as mathematics is concerned. However, be warned that insofar as practice is concerned both
the chi-square interval and the F interval are not said to perform very well and are sensitive to the normality
assumption; use at your peril.

9.5.1  How to do it with R

The sigma.test function in the TeachingDemos package [188] is suited for one-sample problems. Say, for
instance, that Michelson (see Example ??) would like to estimate the variance for his measurements of the
speed of light using the observations in the ﬁrst experiment (see Example BLANK). Recall that we already
investigated the normality of the observations and were satisﬁed. Suppose Michelson would like a 99%
conﬁdence interval for σ2:

> x <- with(morley, Speed[Expt == 1])
> library(TeachingDemos)
> sigma.test(x, conf.level = 0.99)



194                                              CHAPTER 9. ESTIMATION


        One sample Chi-squared test for variance

data:  x
X-squared = 209180, df = 19, p-value < 2.2e-16
alternative hypothesis: true variance is not equal to 1
99 percent confidence interval:
  5421.663 30564.125
sample estimates:
var of x
11009.47

   For the two-sample problem we return to the ToothGrowth data of Example ?? and employ var.test:

> var.test(len ~ supp, data = ToothGrowth, conf.level = 0.99)

        F test to compare two variances

data:  len by supp
F = 0.6386, num df = 29, denom df = 29, p-value
= 0.2331
alternative hypothesis: true ratio of variances is not equal to 1
99 percent confidence interval:
 0.2388183 1.7075900
sample estimates:
ratio of variances
         0.6385951

We see that a 99% conﬁdence interval for σ2OJ/σ2VC is approximately [0.238, 1.708] (round outward, recall
that R orders factor levels alphabetically by default).

9.6  Sample Size and Margin of Error

Sections 9.2 through 9.5 all began the same way: we were given the sample size n and the conﬁdence
coeﬃcient 1 −α, and our task was to ﬁnd a margin of error E so that

                                                      ˆθ ± E is a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for θ.

   Some examples we saw were:
   • E = zα/2σ/ √n, in the one-sample z-interval,
                    √
   • E = tα/2(df = n + m −2)S p  n−1 + m−1, in the two-sample pooled t-interval.

  We already know (we can see in the formulas above) that E decreases as n increases. Now we would like
to use this information to our advantage: suppose that we have a ﬁxed margin of error E, say E = 3, and we
want a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for µ. The question is: how big does n have to be?
   For the case of a population mean the answer is easy: we set up an equation and solve for n.

Example 9.20 (Michelson Speed of Light Data). Returning to the 1879 speed of light data collected by
A. A. Michelson, suppose he would like to estimate the mean speed of light to within 1 km/sec with a 95%
two-sided conﬁdence interval. And for the sake of argument, suppose it is reasonable for Michelson to take
the standard deviation of measurements from his apparatus to be σ = 105. How big would n need to be to
ensure that X ± 1 is a 95% conﬁdence interval for µ?



9.6. SAMPLE SIZE AND MARGIN OF ERROR                                           195


Solution: We know from the conﬁdence interval formula that
                                  σ
                               E = zα/2 · √n,                                        (9.37)

or, solving this equation for n,
                                                                          2                                                  z2α/2 σ2    zα/2σ
                                 n =      =                .                                    (9.38)
                                      E2      E
Now plugging in σ = 105, z0.025 = 1.959964, and E = 1, we get n = 42352.08, that is, Michelson would
need to collect 42,353 observations to achieve such precision!

Remark 9.21. Always round up any decimal values of n, no matter how small the decimal is. Another name
for E is the “maximum error of the estimate”.

   For proportions, recall that the asymptotic formula to estimate p was

                      r ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                                                                       ˆp ± zα/2                   .
                                               n

Reasoning as above we would want

                      r ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                            E = zα/2                   , or                                    (9.39)
                                              n
                                                             ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                                  n = z2α/2               .                                          (9.40)                                           E2

OOPS! Recall that ˆp = Y/n, which would put the variable n on both sides of Equation (9.39). Again, there
are two solutions to the problem.

   1.  If we have a good idea of what p is, say p∗then we can plug it in to get

                                                    p∗(1 −p∗)
                                      n = z2α/2                  .                                   (9.41)                                               E2

   2. Even if we have no idea what p is, we do know from calculus that p(1 −p) ≤1/4 because the function
       f(x) = x(1 −x) is quadratic (so its graph is a parabola which opens downward) with maximum value
      attained at x = 1/2. Therefore, regardless of our choice for p∗the sample size must satisfy

                                                 p∗(1 −p∗)    z2α/2
                                   n = z2α/2       ≤        .                               (9.42)                                            E2      4E2

     The quantity z2α/2/4E2 is large enough to guarantee 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence.

Example 9.22 (Presidential Election 2020). Suppose we are a pollster that would like to estimate the pro-
portion of Ohio likely-voters in support of Democratic candidate Joe Biden. Also suppose that we have
the wherewithal to collect a simple random sample of likely-voters in the great state of Ohio.  If we use a
99% conﬁdence interval, how many voters should we sample to ensure that we are within 0.03 of th true
proportion? Suppose prior polls in the state measured his support level at p = 0.57.

Solution: Incorporating the prior polling data we can set p∗= 0.57 in the equation

                                                  p∗(1 −p∗)
                                    n = z2α/2                                                 (9.43)                                             E2

and plugging in z0.005 = 2.575829, E = 0.03 yields 1016.383, that is, we would need to sample 1017 likely
voters. Maybe we would like to play it safe and set p∗= 0.5; the new required sample size would be 1036.72,
that is, 1037 voters.



196                                              CHAPTER 9. ESTIMATION


Remark 9.23. For very small populations sometimes the value of n obtained from the formula is too big. In
this case we should use the hypergeometric distribution for a sampling model rather than the binomial model.
With this modiﬁcation the formulas change to the following: if N denotes the population size then let

                                                  p∗(1 −p∗)
                         m = z2α/2                                                 (9.44)                                             E2

and the sample size needed to ensure 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence is achieved is

                               m
                                      n =               .                                         (9.45)
                                          1 + m−1N

If we do not have a good value for the estimate p∗then we may use p∗= 1/2.

9.6.1  How to do it with R

We can nearly replicate the analysis for the Michelson speed of light data via the power.t.test function:

> power.t.test(delta = 1, sd = 105, sig.level = 0.05,
+              type = "one.sample", power = 0.5)

     One-sample t test power calculation

              n = 42354
          delta = 1
             sd = 105
      sig.level = 0.05
          power = 0.5
    alternative = two.sided

Note that we set power = 0.5 and we get a value for n almost identical to the one we found earlier.

9.7  Chapter Exercises

Let X1, X2, . . . , Xn be an S RS (n) from a norm(mean = µ, sd = σ) distribution. Find a two-dimensional MLE
for θ = (µ, σ).

                                   Exercise 35

   Find the upper and lower limits for the conﬁdence interval procedure by ﬁnding the roots of f deﬁned by

                                                        p(1 −p)
                                    f(p) = (Y/n −p)2 −z2α/2               .                                                    n

You are going to need the quadratic formula.



Chapter 10

Hypothesis Testing




What do I want them to know?

   • basic terminology and philosophy of the Neyman-Pearson paradigm

   • classical hypothesis tests for the standard one and two sample problems with means, variances, and
      proportions

   • the notion of between versus within group variation and how it plays out with one-way ANOVA

   • the concept of statistical power and its relation to sample size

10.1  Introduction

I spent a week during the summer of 2005 at the University of Nebraska at Lincoln grading Advanced Place-
ment Statistics exams, and while I was there I attended a presentation by Dr. Roxy Peck. At the end of her
talk she described an activity she had used with students to introduce the basic concepts of hypothesis testing.
I was impressed by the activity and have used it in my own classes several times since.

     The instructor (with a box of cookies in hand) enters a class of ﬁfteen or more students and
      produces a brand-new, sealed deck of ordinary playing cards. The instructor asks for a student
      volunteer to break the seal, and then the instructor prominently shuﬄes the deck1 several times
      in front of the class, after which time the students are asked to line up in a row. They are going to
      play a game. Each student will draw a card from the top of the deck, in turn. If the card is black,
      then the lucky student will get a cookie. If the card is red, then the unlucky student will sit down
     empty-handed. Let the game begin.

     The ﬁrst student draws a card: red. There are jeers and outbursts, and the student slinks oﬀto
      his/her chair. (S)he is disappointed, of course, but not really. After all, (s)he had a 50-50 chance
      of getting black, and it did not happen. Oh well.

     The second student draws a card: red, again. There are more jeers, and the second student slips
      away. This student is also disappointed, but again, not so much, because it is probably his/her
     unlucky day. On to the next student.

     The student draws: red again! There are a few wiseguys who yell (happy to make noise, more
      than anything else), but there are a few other students who are not yelling any more – they are
      thinking. This is the third red in a row, which is possible, of course, but what is going on, here?
     They are not quite sure. They are now concentrating on the next card...it is bound to be black,
      right?

   1The jokers are removed before shuﬄing.


                                          197



198                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


     The fourth student draws: red. Hmmm...now there are groans instead of outbursts. A few of
      the students at the end of the line shrug their shoulders and start to make their way back to their
      desk, complaining that the teacher does not want to give away any cookies. There are still some
      students in line though, salivating, waiting for the inevitable black to appear.

     The ﬁfth student draws red. Now it isn’t funny any more. As the remaining students make their
     way back to their seats an uproar ensues, from an entire classroom demanding cookies.

   Keep the preceding experiment in the back of your mind as you read the following sections. When you
have ﬁnished the entire chapter, come back and read this introduction again. All of the mathematical jargon
that follows is connected to the above paragraphs. In the meantime, I will get you started:

Null hypothesis:  it is an ordinary deck of playing cards, shuﬄed thoroughly.

Alternative hypothesis: something funny is going on. Either it is a trick deck of cards, or the instructor is
     doing some fancy shuﬄework.

 [Observed data:] the sequence of draws from the deck, ﬁve reds in a row.

     If it were truly an ordinary, well-shuﬄed deck of cards, the probability of observing zero blacks out of a
sample of size ﬁve (without replacement) from a deck with 26 black cards and 26 red cards would be

> dhyper(0, m = 26, n = 26, k = 5)

[1] 0.02531012

   There are two very important ﬁnal thoughts.  First, everybody gets a cookie in the end. Second, the
students invariably (and aggressively) attempt to get me to open up the deck and reveal the true nature of the
cards. I never do.

10.2  Tests for Proportions

Example 10.1. We have a machine that makes widgets.

   • Under normal operation, about 0.10 of the widgets produced are defective.

   • Go out and purchase a torque converter.

   • Install the torque converter, and observe n = 100 widgets from the machine.

   • Let Y = number of defective widgets observed.

If

   • Y = 0, then the torque converter is great!

   • Y = 4, then the torque converter seems to be helping.

   • Y = 9, then there is not much evidence that the torque converter helps.

   • Y = 17, then throw away the torque converter.

   Let p denote the proportion of defectives produced by the machine. Before the installation of the torque
converter p was 0.10. Then we installed the torque converter. Did p change? Did it go up or down? We use
statistics to decide. Our method is to observe data and construct a 95% conﬁdence interval for p,

                      r ˆp(1 −ˆp)
                                                                       ˆp ± zα/2                   .                                      (10.1)
                                               n

If the conﬁdence interval is



10.2. TESTS FOR PROPORTIONS                                                      199


   • [0.01, 0.05], then we are 95% conﬁdent that 0.01 ≤p ≤0.05, so there is evidence that the torque
      converter is helping.

   • [0.15, 0.19], then we are 95% conﬁdent that 0.15 ≤p ≤0.19, so there is evidence that the torque
      converter is hurting.

   • [0.07, 0.11], then there is not enough evidence to conclude that the torque converter is doing anything
       at all, positive or negative.

10.2.1  Terminology

The null hypothesis H0 is a “nothing” hypothesis, whose interpretation could be that nothing has changed,
there is no diﬀerence, there is nothing special taking place, etc. In Example 10.1 the null hypothesis would
be H0  :  p = 0.10. The alternative hypothesis H1 is the hypothesis that something has changed, in this
case, H1  : p , 0.10. Our goal is to statistically test the hypothesis H0  : p = 0.10 versus the alternative
H1 : p , 0.10. Our procedure will be:

   1. Go out and collect some data, in particular, a simple random sample of observations from the machine.

   2. Suppose that H0 is true and construct a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for p.

   3.  If the conﬁdence interval does not cover p = 0.10, then we reject H0. Otherwise, we fail to reject H0.

Remark 10.2. Every time we make a decision it is possible to be wrong, and there are two possible mistakes
we can make. We have committed a

Type I Error  if we reject H0 when in fact H0 is true. This would be akin to convicting an innocent person
      for a crime (s)he did not commit.

Type II Error  if we fail to reject H0 when in fact H1 is true. This is analogous to a guilty person escaping
      conviction.

Type I Errors are usually considered worse2, and we design our statistical procedures to control the probability
of making such a mistake. We deﬁne the

                          signiﬁcance level of the test = P(Type I Error) = α.                      (10.2)

We want α to be small which conventionally means, say, α = 0.05, α = 0.01, or α = 0.005 (but could mean
anything, in principle).

   • The rejection region (also known as the critical region) for the test is the set of sample values which
     would result in the rejection of H0.  For Example 10.1, the rejection region would be all possible
     samples that result in a 95% conﬁdence interval that does not cover p = 0.10.
   • The above example with H1 : p , 0.10 is called a two-sided test. Many times we are interested in a
      one-sided test, which would look like H1 : p < 0.10 or H1 : p > 0.10.

  We are ready for tests of hypotheses for one proportion. We know from Section 9.4 that when H0 : p = p0
is true and n is large,
                                                   ˆp ∼norm(mean = p0, sd = p p0(1 −p0)/n),                          (10.3)

approximately, and the approximation gets better as the sample size gets bigger. Another way to write this is

                                                               ˆp −p0
                      Z =            ∼norm(mean = 0, sd = 1).                        (10.4)
                   p p0(1 −p0)/n

   Assumptions for a valid test:

   2There is no mathematical diﬀerence between the errors, however. The bottom line is that we choose one type of error to control with
an iron ﬁst, and we try to minimize the probability of the other type. That being said, null hypotheses are often by design to correspond
to the “simpler” model, so it is often easier to analyze (and thereby control) the probabilities associated with Type I Errors.



200                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


                           H0     Ha       Rejection Region
                             p = p0   p > p0   z > zα
                             p = p0   p < p0   z < −zα
                             p = p0   p , p0     |z| > zα/2


                   Table 10.1: Hypothesis tests, population proportion, large sample.


   • A simple random sample from a Bernoulli population

   • The sample size n is large

   • need at least 15 successes and at least 15 failures

Example 10.3. Suppose p = the proportion of students who are admitted to the graduate school of the Uni-
versity of California at Berkeley, and suppose that a public relations oﬃcer boasts that UCB has historically
had a 40% acceptance rate for its graduate school. Consider the data stored in the table UCBAdmissions
from 1973. Assuming these observations constituted a simple random sample, are they consistent with the
oﬃcer’s claim, or do they provide evidence that the acceptance rate was signiﬁcantly less than 40%? Use an
α = 0.01 signiﬁcance level.

   Our null hypothesis in this problem is H0 : p = 0.4 and the alternative hypothesis is H1 : p < 0.4. We
reject the null hypothesis if ˆp is too small, that is, if

                                                                           ˆp −0.4
                                     √0.4(1 −0.4)/n < −zα,                                    (10.5)

where α = 0.01 and −z0.01 is

> -qnorm(0.99)

[1] -2.326348

   That is, −z0.01 ≈2.326. Our only remaining task is to ﬁnd the value of the test statistic and see where it
falls relative to the critical value. We can ﬁnd the number of applicants admitted and not admitted to the UCB
graduate school with the following.

> xtabs(Freq ~ Admit, data = UCBAdmissions)

Admit
Admitted Rejected
    1755     2771

So a point estimate for p would be ˆp = 1755/(1755 + 2771) ≈0.388. Next we calculate the value of the z
test statistic:

> phat <- 1755/(1755 + 2771)
> (phat - 0.4)/sqrt(0.4 * 0.6/(1755 + 2771))

[1] -1.680919

With z ≈−1.681 our test statistic is not less than −2.326, so it does not fall into the critical region. Therefore,
we fail to reject the null hypothesis that the true proportion of students admitted to graduate school is less
than 40% and say that the observed data are consistent with the oﬃcer’s claim at the α = 0.01 signiﬁcance
level.



10.2. TESTS FOR PROPORTIONS                                                      201


Example 10.4. We are going to do Example 10.3 all over again. Everything will be exactly the same except
for one change. Suppose we choose signiﬁcance level α = 0.05 instead of α = 0.01. Are the 1973 data
consistent with the oﬃcer’s claim?

   Our null and alternative hypotheses are the same. Our observed test statistic is the same: it was approxi-
mately −1.681. But notice that our critical value has changed: α = 0.05 and −z0.05 is

> -qnorm(0.95)

[1] -1.644854

Our test statistic is less than −1.645 so it now falls into the critical region! We now reject the null hypothesis
and conclude that the 1973 data provide evidence that the true proportion of students admitted to the graduate
school of UCB in 1973 was signiﬁcantly less than 40%. The data are not consistent with the oﬃcer’s claim
at the α = 0.05 signiﬁcance level.
   What is going on, here?  If we choose α = 0.05 then we reject the null hypothesis, but if we choose
α = 0.01 then we fail to reject the null hypothesis. Our ﬁnal conclusion seems to depend on our selection of
the signiﬁcance level. This is bad; for a particular test, we never know whether our conclusion would have
been diﬀerent if we had chosen a diﬀerent signiﬁcance level.
   Or do we?
    Clearly, for some signiﬁcance levels we reject, and for some signiﬁcance levels we do not. Where is the
boundary? That is, what is the signiﬁcance level for which we would reject at any signiﬁcance level bigger,
and we would fail to reject at any signiﬁcance level smaller? This boundary value has a special name: it is
called the p-value of the test.

Deﬁnition 10.5 (Observed signiﬁcance level.). The p-value, or observed signiﬁcance level, of a hypothesis
test is the probability when the null hypothesis is true of obtaining the observed value of the test statistic
(such as ˆp) or values more extreme – meaning, in the direction of the alternative hypothesis3.

Example 10.6. Calculate the p-value for the tests in Examples 10.3 and 10.4.

   The p-value for this test is the probability of obtaining a z-score equal to our observed test statistic (which
had z ≈−1.681) or more extreme, which in this example is less than the observed test statistic. In other
words, we want to know the area under a standard normal curve on the interval (−∞, −1.680919]. We can
get this easily with

> pnorm(-1.680919)

[1] 0.04638932

  We see that the p-value is strictly between the signiﬁcance levels α = 0.01 and α = 0.05. This makes
sense: it has to be bigger than α = 0.01 (otherwise we would have rejected H0 in Example 10.3) and it must
also be smaller than α = 0.05 (otherwise we would not have rejected H0 in Example 10.4). Indeed, p-values
are a characteristic indicator of whether or not we would have rejected at assorted signiﬁcance levels, and
for this reason a statistician will often skip the calculation of critical regions and critical values entirely. If
(s)he knows the p-value, then (s)he knows immediately whether or not (s)he would have rejected at any given
signiﬁcance level.
   Thus, another way to phrase our signiﬁcance test procedure is: we will reject H0 at the α-level of signiﬁ-
cance if the p-value is less than α.

Remark 10.7.  If we have two populations with proportions p1 and p2 then we can test the null hypothesis
H0 : p1 = p2. In that which follows,

   3Bickel and Doksum [18] state the deﬁnition particularly well: the p-value is “the smallest level of signiﬁcance α at which an
experimenter using the test statistic T would reject H0 on the basis of the observed sample outcome x”.

How to do it with R

The following code conducts the one-sample test for the U.C. Berkeley data:

> prop.test(1755, n = 4526, p = 0.4, alternative = "less",
+            correct = FALSE)

        1-sample proportions test without continuity
        correction

data:  1755 out of 4526, null probability 0.4
X-squared = 2.8255, df = 1, p-value = 0.04639
alternative hypothesis: true p is less than 0.4
95 percent confidence interval:
 0.0000000 0.3997361
sample estimates:
        p
0.3877596

To make the plot in Figure ??, ﬁrst store the hypothesis test and then plot the resulting htest object:

> temp <- prop.test(1755, n = 4526, p = 0.4, alternative = "less",
+            correct = FALSE)
> plot(temp)

The default signiﬁcance level for plotting htest objects is α = 0.05, but the default can be changed via the
conf.level argument to the hypothesis test; set it equal to 1 −α.

Remark 10.8. In all of the above we set correct = FALSE to tell the computer that we did not want to use
Yates’ continuity correction (reference BLANK). It is customary to use Yates when the expected frequency
of successes is less than 10. (reference BLANK) You can use it all of the time, but you will have a decrease
in power. For large samples the correction does not matter.

   For the two-sample problem, consider Titanic survival data broken down by gender (and for the moment
ridiculously suspend all disbelief that somehow passengers on the 1912 maiden voyage of the Olympic-class
ocean liner RMS Titanic could possibly be a representative sample of any population other than themselves):

> addmargins(xtabs(Freq ~ Sex + Survived, data = Titanic),
+            margin=2)

        Survived
Sex        No  Yes  Sum
  Male   1364  367 1731
  Female  126  344  470

     If we set p1 as the proportion of Male who died (Survived == "No") and p2 the proportion of Female
who died then we can test whether the proportions are the same this way:

> prop.test(x = c(1364, 126), n = c(1731, 470), correct = FALSE)

        2-sample test for equality of proportions
        without continuity correction

We reject the null hypothesis. See also Section ??.

10.3.1  For Means

Here, X1, X2, . . . , Xn are a S RS (n) from a norm(mean = µ, sd = σ) distribution. We would like to test
H0 : µ = µ0.
   Case A: Suppose σ is known. Then under H0,

                         X −µ0
                        Z =      ∼norm(mean = 0, sd = 1).                            (10.7)                            σ/ √n

                           H0     Ha      Rejection Region
                           µ = µ0  µ > µ0   z > zα
                           µ = µ0  µ < µ0   z < −zα
                           µ = µ0  µ , µ0     |z| > zα/2


                      Table 10.3: Hypothesis tests, population mean, large sample.

                           H0     Ha      Rejection Region
                           µ = µ0  µ > µ0    t > tα(n −1)
                           µ = µ0  µ < µ0    t < −tα(n −1)
                           µ = µ0  µ , µ0     |t| > tα/2(n −1)


                     Table 10.4: Hypothesis tests, population mean, small sample.


   Case B: When σ is unknown, under H0,

                             X −µ0
                             T =       ∼t(df = n −1).                                  (10.8)                                   S/ √n

Remark 10.9.  If σ is unknown but n is large then we can use the z-test.

Example 10.10 (Results from an Experiment on Plant Growth). We studied the PlantGrowth data in Ex-
ample 9.13 where we constructed a 95% conﬁdence interval for µ, the population mean yield. Recall that the
population standard deviation was known to be σ = 0.7 g, and the data appeared approximately normal.
   Suppose now that we would like to know whether the data provide evidence that the true mean yield is
diﬀerent from 4.8 g at the α = 0.05 signiﬁcance level. The null hypothesis would be H0 : µ = 4.8, and the
alternative hypothesis would be Ha : µ , 4.8. Given the shape of the distribution and the population standard
deviation being known, this is clearly a job for the z-test.
   From Table 10.3 our rejection region (recall that zα/2 ≈1.96 would be |z| > 1.96, or in other words, we
reject H0 if z > 1.96 or if z < −1.96, where our test statistic z is

                                     x −µ0   5.073 −4.8
                                      z =     =    √    ≈2.136.                             σ/ √n     0.7/  30

We see, then, that our test statistic does indeed fall into the rejection region, so we will reject the null hypoth-
esis at the α = 0.05 signiﬁcance level and conclude that these data do indeed provide evidence that the true
population mean yield is diﬀerent from 4.8 g.
   The p-value for this problem is the probability that a standard normal Z is more extreme (in either direc-
tion) than what we observed (z ≈2.136), which would be double the upper tail probability 1 - pnorm(2.136),
that is,

> 2*(1 - pnorm(2.136))

[1] 0.03267941

Since our p-value is less than our signiﬁcance level α = 0.05, all signs are pointing to the same place: we
should reject the null hypothesis that µ = 4.8. A visual display of these ideas is shown in Figure 10.2.

Remark 10.11. But wait; there is more. In Example 9.13 we calculated a 95% two-sided conﬁdence interval
for µ, and we were 95% conﬁdent that the true mean was covered by the interval [4.82, 5.33], which we
notice does not cover the null hypothesized value µ0 = 4.8. So here is yet another sign that the two-sided
hypothesis test will reject H0 : µ = 4.8 at signiﬁcance level α = 1 −0.95 = 0.05. There is a deep connection,
a duality, between conﬁdence intervals and hypothesis tests.

of our Z test statistic is norm(mean = 4.8, sd = 0.7/  30), which matches the bell curve shown above. The critical region
is marked by the shaded blue area, and the observed value of sample mean x = 5.073 is denoted by a dashed vertical
green line. That value of x translates to a z-score of 2.136, shown in the lower horizontal axes, along with the critical
value zα/2 ≈1.96. The p-value p ≈0.0327 is marked by the area enclosed by the green box, and we see it is less than the
signiﬁcance level α = 0.05.

   The quantity σ/ √n, when σ is known, is called the standard error of the sample mean. In general, if we
have an estimator ˆθ then σˆθ is called the standard error of ˆθ. We usually need to estimate σˆθ with σˆθ.                                                        b
Example 10.12 (Michelson Speed of Light Data). Recall Example 9.16 where we computed a 99% two-sided
t-interval for µ, the unknown true mean speed of light based on Michelson’s measurements in the morley data
frame. We focused on the 20 observations in the ﬁrst experiment, which is a small sample, but we checked
normality with a qqPlot and were satisﬁed.
   Now, suppose that Michelson asserts that the true mean speed of light is greater than 299,845 km/sec4.
Do the data from the ﬁrst 20 runs support Michelson’s claim at the α = 0.01 signiﬁcance level? Our null
hypothesis will be H0 : µ = 845, the alternative will be H0 : µ > 845, and we will be using Student’s t-test.
Table 10.4 tells us that we should reject H0 if our test statistic t is greater than t0.01(df = n −1), which is
qt(0.99, df = 19) ≈2.54. It remains only to calculate the observed value of our test statistic, which is

                                  x −µ0    909 −845
                                                t =     =      √   ≈2.727796.                                     s/ √n   104.926/  20

This test statistic falls in the rejection region, so Michelson rejects the null hypothesis H0  : µ = 845. He
infers that the data from the ﬁrst 20 runs provide statistically signiﬁcant evidence at the α = 0.01 level to
conclude that the true mean speed of light is greater than 299,845 km/sec.
   The p-value for this test would be the probability that a T ∼t(df = 19) variable is larger than 2.727796,
which is

> 1 - pt(2.727796, df = 19)

[1] 0.006681162

Here, we see that the p-value is less than α = 0.01, further conﬁrming that Michelson’s conclusion should be
to reject the null hypothesis.

Remark 10.13. Hold the phone.  In Remark 10.11 we said that there was a duality between conﬁdence
intervals and hypothesis tests, and we pointed out that in Example ?? the two-sided 95% conﬁdence interval
we computed, [4.82, 5.33], for the µ in that problem didn’t cover the null hypothesized value µ0 = 4.8, so it
stood to reason when the hypothesis test rejected H0 : µ = 4.8 at the α = 0.05 signiﬁcance level.
    Yet, in Example ?? we have computed a two-sided 99% conﬁdence interval for Michelson’s µ, and the
interval turns out to be [841, 977]. This interval covers µ0 = 845. How is it that Michelson rejects H0 : µ =
845 at the α = 0.01 signiﬁcance level?
   The seeming inconsistency is due to the mixing and matching of one- and two-sided conﬁdence intervals
with hypothesis tests.  In Examples ?? and ??, both interval and test were two-sided, and the inferences
matched. In Examples ?? and ??, the interval was two-sided, but the test was one-sided, and the inferences
did not match.
    In general, a two-sided test rejects when the two-sided interval doesn’t cover µ0, and the same holds
true for one-sided tests/intervals. If you mix and match then the correspondence is not guaranteed. This is
explored more in Exercise 38.


How to do it with R

We will use the z.test function in the TeachingDemos package.

> library(TeachingDemos)
> z.test(PlantGrowth$weight, mu = 4.8, sd = 0.7,
+        alternative = "two.sided")

Compare the output here to what we computed by hand in Example 10.10. Just as with prop.test, we can
make the plot in Figure 10.2 by saving the results of the z.test and plot-ting it.
   For Michelson’s t.test we can do

> x <- with(morley, Speed[Expt == 1])
> t.test(x, mu = 845, conf.level = 0.99, alternative = "greater")

        One Sample t-test

data:  x
t = 2.7278, df = 19, p-value = 0.006681
alternative hypothesis: true mean is greater than 845
99 percent confidence interval:
 849.4182      Inf
sample estimates:
mean of x
      909

Notice that the one-sided 99% conﬁdence interval extends [849, ∞); this interval does not cover µ0 = 845,
so in Remark ?? there is no need to hold the phone after all.
