10.3.2  For Variance

Here, X1, X2, . . . , Xn are a S RS (n) from a norm(mean = µ, sd = σ) distribution. We would like to test
H0 : σ2 = σ20. We know that under H0,
                                        (n −1)S 2
                            X2 =         ∼chisq(df = n −1).
                                   σ2

    Fair warning to the reader that this test is known to be sensitive to departures from normality.


How to do it with R

The sigma.test function in the TeachingDemos package [188] implements this test. We used it to construct
a conﬁdence interval for σ2 in Section 9.5.1, and the hypothesis test functionality extends in the obvious way.
The user may either specify the null hypothesized value sigma = σ0 or sigmasq = σ20. For instance, in the
morley data we could test the null hypothesis H0 : σ = 79, equivalently H0 : σ2 = 792, against a two-sided
alternative with:



10.4. TWO-SAMPLE TESTS FOR MEANS AND VARIANCES                               209


                  H0      Ha        Rejection Region
                  σ2 = σ20  σ2 > σ20   X2 > χ2α(n −1)
                  σ2 = σ20  σ2 < σ20   X2 < χ21−α(n −1)
                  σ2 = σ20  σ2 , σ20   X2 > χ2α/2(n −1) or X2 < χ21−α/2(n −1)


                  Table 10.5: Hypothesis tests, population variance, normal population.


> sigma.test(morley$Speed, sigma = 79)

Note that conﬁdence intervals returned by sigma.test are invariably for σ2, not σ.


10.4  Two-Sample Tests for Means and Variances

10.4.1  Independent Samples

The basic idea for this section is the following. We have X ∼norm(mean = µX, sd = σX) and Y ∼
norm(mean = µY, sd = σY) distributed independently. We would like to know whether X and Y come from
the same population distribution, in other words, we would like to know:

                                     Does X d= Y?                                         (10.9)

where the symbol d= means equality of probability distributions. Since both X and Y are normal, we may
rephrase the question:
                               Does µX = µY and σX = σY?                               (10.10)

Suppose ﬁrst that we do not know the values of σX and σY, but we know that they are equal, σX = σY. Our
test would then simplify to H0 : µX = µY. We collect data X1, X2, ..., Xn and Y1, Y2, ..., Ym, both simple
random samples of size n and m from their respective normal distributions. Then under H0 (that is, assuming
H0 is true) we have µX = µY, or rewriting, µX −µY = 0, so

                     X −Y    X −Y −(µX −µY)
                  T =        =                ∼t(df = n + m −2).                (10.11)
                q 1    1     q 1    1
                       S p   n + m      S p   n + m

A test based on this statistic is the “Pooled t-test”.
    In the much more common scenario where the variances are not known to be equal, we follow Section 9.3
and use the Welch adjustment to the degrees of freedom for the distribution of the statistic

                               X −Y
                             T =          ∼t(df = r),                               (10.12)
                      q S 2X    S Y2                                +                                                       n   m

where the degrees of freedom r is chosen so that the statistic has nice properties. See Equation (9.24) for the
formula. A test based on this statistic is called the “Welch Two Sample t-test”. The Welch test is the default.

Remark 10.14.  If the values of σX and σY are known, then we can plug them in to our statistic:

                                   X −Y
                               Z =                         ;                                  (10.13)
                       q
                                       σ2X/n + σ2Y/m

the result will have a norm(mean = 0, sd = 1) distribution when H0 : µX = µY is true.



210                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


                         H0       Ha        Rejection Region
                         µD = D0  µD > D0    t > tα(n + m −2)
                         µD = D0  µD < D0    t < −tα(n + m −2)
                         µD = D0  µD , D0     |t| > tα/2(n + m −2)


                    Table 10.6: Rejection regions, diﬀerence in means, Pooled t-test.

              H0       Ha         Rejection Region
              σ2X = σ2Y   σ2X > σ2Y     f > fα(n −1, m −1)
              σ2X = σ2Y   σ2X < σ2Y     f < f1−α(n −1, m −1)
              σ2X = σ2Y   σ2X , σ2Y     f < f1−α/2(n −1, m −1) or f > fα/2(n −1, m −1)


                        Table 10.7: Rejection regions, ratio of variances, F-test.


Remark 10.15. Even if the values of σX and σY are not known, if both n and m are large then we can plug
in the sample estimates and the result will have approximately a norm(mean = 0, sd = 1) distribution when
H0 : µX = µY is true.
                                   X −Y
                               Z =                            .                                   (10.14)
                        q  2        2
                                        S X/n + S Y/m

Remark 10.16.  It is often helpful to construct side-by-side boxplots and other visual displays in concert with
the hypothesis test. This gives a visual comparison of the samples and helps to identify departures from the
test’s assumptions—such as outliers.

Remark 10.17. WATCH YOUR ASSUMPTIONS.

   • The normality assumption can be relaxed as long as the population distributions are not highly skewed.

   • The equal variance assumption can be relaxed as long as both sample sizes n and m are large. However,
        if one (or both) samples is small, then the test does not perform well; we should instead use the methods
      of Chapter 14.

   For a nonparametric alternative to the two-sample t-test see Chapter 13.
   For the two-sample variance problem, we rely on the work we already did with conﬁdence intervals in
Section 9.5. There we saw that

                             σ2YS 2X                     F =      ∼f(df1 = n −1, df2 = m −1).                        (10.15)
                             σ2XS Y2

Then when H0 : σ2X = σ2Y is true, the statistic

                               S 2X                      F =    ∼f(df1 = n −1, df2 = m −1).                          (10.16)
                               S Y2

which leads to Table 10.7.


How to do it with R

Refer to Section 9.3 where we used t.test to construct conﬁdence intervals for the diﬀerence in means.
Each of those intervals extends to a one- or two-sided hypothesis test in the straightforward way. Most of the
time we are interested in whether the means are the same (µX = µY), but on the rare occasion that we need to
test whether the diﬀerence in means is some constant D0 , 0, we can specify so via the mu = D0 argument to



10.4. TWO-SAMPLE TESTS FOR MEANS AND VARIANCES                               211


                         H0       Ha        Rejection Region
                          µD = D0  µD > D0    t > tα(nD −1)
                          µD = D0  µD < D0    t < −tα(nD −2)
                          µD = D0  µD , D0     |t| > tα/2(nD −1)


                    Table 10.8: Rejection regions, diﬀerence in means, Paired t-test.


t.test. The Welch-Aspin test (no equal variance assumption) is performed by default, but we can call the
Pooled t-test with var.equal = TRUE if we like.
   The two-sample variances test is done with var.test. See Section 9.3 for an example with the ToothGrowth
data.

10.4.2  Paired Samples

Often times we have repeated measurements on a single experimental unit. For instance, maybe we would
like to decide between two golf clubs which is better, so we recruit a bunch of buddies to go to the golf course
with us, to collect data on golf ball driving distances for each club. It would be silly to have each buddy hit a
single golf ball and go home—we would need twice as many buddies! No, we should have each person hit
a ball apiece with both clubs. Indeed, we should ask our friends to hit a whole bunch of golf balls with each
club, giving us as much data as we can get, but suppose for the sake of argument that our buddies are in a
hurry and only have time to hit one ball with each club apiece.
  We will observe X1, X2, ..., Xn and Y1, Y2, ..., Yn, driving distances, and suppose that we conduct our
experiment in such a way that our golfer buddies are independent of one another. But there is a problem,
because even if the X’s are independent of each other and the Y’s are all independent, the pair (X1, Y1 are
not independent. If I tell you that Tiger Woods hit X1 then it will give you a lot of information about Y1, and
similarly about Y2 if I tell you that X2 was hit by somebody who has never golfed before (me).
   But all is not lost. Let us compute the diﬀerence of each pair: D1 = X1 −Y1, D2 = X2 −Y2, and so on, up
to Dn = Xn −Yn. Then the diﬀerences D1, . . . , Dn are a simple random sample, with mean

                       µD = E (D) = E(X −Y) = E X −E Y = µX −µY,                      (10.17)

and we can estimate, construct conﬁdence intervals, and perform hypothesis tests on the parameter µD via
single-sample procedures on the D’s, subject to the same assumptions and limitations.
  A popular procedure to use is the “Paired t-test” which is appropriate for large samples or small samples
when the population of diﬀerences is normally distributed.  See Table 10.8 for how the hypotheses and
rejection regions are deﬁned.

Example 10.18 (Student’s sleep data). The sleep data measure the eﬀect of two soporiﬁc (sleep-inducing)
drugs by the increase in hours of sleep compared to control on 10 patients. The extra sleep measured while
taking the ﬁrst drug we will denoted by extra.1 and the extra sleep measured while on the second drug will
be denoted extra.2. Supposing that the 10 patients constitute a simple random sample, we would like to
know if there is any diﬀerence between the two soporiﬁc drugs with respect to mean extra sleep obtained.


How to do it with R

How the Paired t-test is done depends on how the data are formatted, whether in “long” or “wide” format.
The sleep data frame from the included datasets package are already in long format, that is, like this:

> head(sleep)

  extra group ID
1   0.7     1  1



212                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


2  -1.6     1  2
3  -0.2     1  3
4  -1.2     1  4
5  -0.1     1  5
6   3.4     1  6

The extra variable (length 20) records the extra sleep for each patient, the group variable denotes which
soporiﬁc drug was used (1 or 2), and the ID variable identiﬁes the patient being measured (from 1 to 10).
When data are in this format the Paired t-test looks like this:

> t.test(extra ~ group, data = sleep, paired = TRUE)

        Paired t-test

data:  extra by group
t = -4.0621, df = 9, p-value = 0.002833
alternative hypothesis: true mean difference is not equal to 0
95 percent confidence interval:
 -2.4598858 -0.7001142
sample estimates:
mean difference
          -1.58

Here we would reject the null hypothesis that the mean extra sleep for the two soporiﬁc drugs is the same.
However, often times paired data are not in long format by default, rather they are in wide format with the
ﬁrst measurement in one column and the second measurement in another column alongside the ﬁrst one. The
help ﬁle for t.test shows how to convert the default sleep data frame into wide format:

> sleep2 <- reshape(sleep, direction = "wide",
+                   idvar = "ID", timevar = "group")
> head(sleep2)

  ID extra.1 extra.2
1  1     0.7     1.9
2  2    -1.6     0.8
3  3    -0.2     1.1
4  4    -1.2     0.1
5  5    -0.1    -0.1
6  6     3.4     4.4

The sleep2 data frame has 10 rows, ID denoting the patient, extra.1 the extra sleep for the ﬁrst drug,
extra.2 for the second drug. Now the syntax for Paired t-test is a bit diﬀerent:

> t.test(Pair(extra.1, extra.2) ~ 1, data = sleep2)

        Paired t-test

data:  Pair(extra.1, extra.2)
t = -4.0621, df = 9, p-value = 0.002833
alternative hypothesis: true mean difference is not equal to 0
95 percent confidence interval:
 -2.4598858 -0.7001142
sample estimates:
mean difference
          -1.58



10.5. OTHER HYPOTHESIS TESTS                                                     213


Aside from the data: line the output is exactly the same. But wait, we have gotten ahead of ourselves,
because we failed to check our assumptions, namely, that this small sample (n = 10) of diﬀerences is normally
distributed. The wide format of the data is more convenient to check this with a qqPlot (not shown, see
Exercise ??).

10.5  Other Hypothesis Tests

We have yet discussed only a small sample5 of hypothesis tests available to the statistician. We will see
several more before we are through, but in the meantime, here are a couple more.

10.5.1  Shapiro-Wilk Normality Test

Most of the small-sample procedures we have studied thus far require that the target population be normally
distributed. In later chapters we will be assuming that errors from a proposed model are normally distributed,
and one method we will use to assess the model’s adequacy will be to investigate the plausibility of that
assumption. So, you see, determining whether or not a random sample comes from a normal distribution is
an important problem for us. We have already learned graphical methods to address the question (histograms,
qqPlots), but here we will study a formal hypothesis test of the same. We will be given a simple random
sample X1, X2, . . . , Xn,, considered observations of a random variable X, and our null hypothesis will be

                           H0 : X ∼norm(mean = µ, sd = σ),                            (10.18)

where µ and σ are not speciﬁed, and the alternative hypothesis is simply Ha : not H0.
  We do not specify the parameters µ and σ because at the moment we do not really care about the center
or spread of the distribution, instead, we care about its shape. A hypothesis test like this, that is not focused
on the value of this or that parameter, is a member of large class of nonparametric methods, the subject of
Chapter ??.
   The test statistic we will use is Wilk’s W, which looks like this:

                                   Pni=1 aiX(i) 2
                     W =                        ,                                   (10.19)
                                  Pni=1(Xi −X)2

where the x(i)’s are the order statistics (see Section BLANK) and the constants ai form the components of a
vector a1×n deﬁned by
                                     mTV−1
                                   a = √                      ,                                   (10.20)
                                  mTV−1V−1m
where mn×1 and Vn×n are the mean and covariance matrix, respectively, of the order statistics from an
mvnorm (mean = 0, sigma = I) distribution. This test statistic W was published in 1965 by (you guessed
it): Shapiro and Wilk [187]. In contrast to most other test statistics we know, we reject H0 if W is too small.


How to do it with R

The morley data set has a Speed variable containing Michelson’s 100 measurements of the speed of light.
We check normality of the data by way of the shapiro.test function.

> with(morley, shapiro.test(Speed))

        Shapiro-Wilk normality test

data:  Speed
W = 0.98807, p-value = 0.5137

  5No pun intended. OK, maybe moderately intended, p = 0.07.



214                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


    In this case we do not reject the null hypothesis that the Speed data are normally distributed. However, it
is important to note that we have not proved that the data are normal, nor could we ever possibly prove such
a thing via the Shapiro-Wilk test. Indeed, it is here that our carefully worded hypothesis testing language
comes into play. We make exactly one of two (2) decisions: we either reject the null hypothesis, or we fail
to reject. In this example, we failed to reject, and an appropriate interpretation would be, “These data are
consistent with a simple random sample from a normal distribution,” or some other wishy-washy remark.
  On the other hand, had the Shapiro-Wilk test come back highly signiﬁcant, say, p = 0.0001, we would
reject the null hypothesis and forcefully declare from the rooftop, “These data provide highly signiﬁcant
evidence that the population distribution of X is not normal,” and go on about our business.

10.5.2  Kolmogorov-Smirnov Goodness-of-Fit Test

Considering what led us to the Shapiro-Wilk test of normality, we may ﬁnd ourselves wanting to know
whether a given random sample of data follow any one of a cornucopia of possible population distributions,
not necessarily normal. The Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) test permits testing the ﬁt of a speciﬁed candidate
population distribution, which we assume to be continuous6. Alternatively, the KS test can be used in two-
sample problems to test the null hypothesis that X sample data and Y sample data come from the same
(continuous) distribution, regardless of what that population distribution may be. Consequently, the KS test
is another nonparametric test; see Chapter ??.


How to do it with R

> with(randu, ks.test(x, "punif"))

        Asymptotic one-sample Kolmogorov-Smirnov test

data:  x
D = 0.055524, p-value = 0.1697
alternative hypothesis: two-sided

10.6  Analysis of Variance

Given samples X1, . . . , Xn1 ∼norm(µ1, σ) and Y1, . . . , Yn2 ∼norm(µ2, σ), if we wish to test the null hypothe-
sis H0 : µ1 = µ2 we can use the (Pooled) two-sample t-test in Section 10.4.1.
   But now we have k ≥3 independent normal samples with unknown means µ1, µ2, . . . , µk and common
variance σ2. We would like to test the null hypothesis

                                H0 : µ1 = µ2 = · · · = µk                                  (10.21)

against all possible alternatives. We will write the observed data like this:

                             X11, . . . , Xn11  ,  X12, . . . , Xn22  , . . . ,  X1k, . . . , Xnkk  ,

and we will write our model in this way:

                                Xij = µ + τj + ϵij,     j = 1, . . . , k,    i = 1, . . . , nj,

where

   • N = Pkj=1 nj is the total sample size,

   • µ is the grand mean,

   6Only as far as base R is concerned.  For discrete distributions or mixed discrete/continuous distributions see the KSgeneral
package.



10.6. ANALYSIS OF VARIANCE                                                       215


   • τj is the eﬀect of treatment  j, for j = 1, . . . , k,
   • Pkj=1 τj = 0,

   •  ϵij’s are a S RS (N) from a norm(mean = 0, sd = σ) distribution.

Example 10.19 (Chicken weights by feed type). The chickwts dataset records the weights of 71 chicks
according to the feed they were given, such as sunﬂower seeds, horsebean, meatmeal, etc. (There were six
diﬀerent feeds used in the study.) We displayed side-by-side boxplots of these data in Figure ??. Now, we are
interested to know whether the feed types diﬀer in terms of a chick’s mean weight. There are six feeds; do
all feeds lead to the same average weight? Maybe one or more feeds have an average weight above or below
the others.
    In symbols, we are looking to test the null hypothesis

                               H0 : µ1 = µ2 = · · · = µ6,                                 (10.22)

but making the connection to our model we write µ1 = µ + τ1, µ2 = µ + τ2, and so on, up to µ6 = µ + τ6, so
that the hypothesis BLANK corresponds to the equivalent hypothesis

                              H0 : τ1 = τ2 = · · · = τ6 = 0.                               (10.23)

   Idea: If the groups have means that are far apart, the data will look like the top graph in Figure ??. But
if the groups have means that are close together, then the data will look like the bottom graph in Figure ??.


Important Quantities

We need some notation. Denote the sample mean of the jth group by

                                                                                nj
                                          1 X                                             X· j =        Xi j
                                          n j                                                                    i=1

for j = 1, 2, . . . , k, and the sample variance of the jth group by

                                                                             nj
                                                2     1 X                                S ·j =           (Xi j −X·j)2                                               nj −1                                                                 i=1

for j = 1, 2, . . . , k, and the (sample) grand mean by

                                                                      k   n j
                                         1 X X                                                     X·· =           Xi j.
                              N
                                                                 j=1 i=1

  Now we will deﬁne S S T, the Treatment Sum of Squares, by

                                                                 k
               X                                 S S T =    nj(X· j −X··)2,                                 (10.24)
                                                             j=1

which we will use to measure variation in treatment means, or between group variability. We will next deﬁne
S S E, the Error Sum of Squares, by
                                                                   k
               X           2                                  S S E =    (nj −1)S j,                                  (10.25)
                                                               j=1

which we will use to measure variation around treatment means, or within group variability.
   Next, we will need the Mean Square for Treatment MS T and the Mean Square for Error MS E deﬁned
respectively by
                                     S S T           S S E
                      MS T =      MS E =                                          k −1,       N −k,



216                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING





                  0.4

                  0.2                               density

                  0.0
                     0    5    10   15   20   25

                                 response





                  0.4

                  0.2                               density

                  0.0
                    0        5       10       15

                                 response




Figure 10.3: A plot of between group versus within group variation. Both graphs represent a simulated response
variable from three groups.  In the top graph, the group means are far apart, so the between group variation is large
relative to the within group variation. In the bottom graph, the group means are relatively close to each other, so the
within group variation is large relative to the between group variation. It was the genius of Sir R.A. Fisher which realized
that to detect a diﬀerence in means, what we should be studying is the variation between/within groups, hence the term
analysis of variance.



10.6. ANALYSIS OF VARIANCE                                                       217


and ﬁnally the F ratio
                               MS T
                                F =           .
                               MS E
     If the population means were diﬀerent, we would expect the sample means to be far apart, so the between
group MS T would be BIG, and the within group MS E would be small, so the F ratio would be

                             MS T
                             F =     →BIG.
                             MS E

Now, it turns out that when the null hypothesis is true, that is, when τ1 = · · · = τk = 0, we can prove that

                        F ∼f(df1 = k −1, df2 = N −k).                             (10.26)

Our ANOVA procedure, then, will be to collect data in accordance with the ANOVA assumptions, calculate
the test statistic F, and reject H0 when F is too BIG, that is, larger than fα(k −1, N −k).


Checking ANOVA assumptions

We can distill the underlying assumptions of our ANOVA model into three conditions: (1) each group should
be an independent, random sample, which (2) is normally distributed, with (3) the same (homogeneous)
variance in all groups.
  We usually address whether or not (1) is satisﬁed in a given problem by inspecting the study design.
  We can check (2) visually with multiple qq-plots or histograms, one for each group, and we can formally
test normality by groups with shapiro.test, see Exercise ??. But be warned that in a given problem there
may be too many groups and too few observations per group to judge normality in each group separately with
any power. It is often better to wait until after ﬁtting the model to examine the residuals all at the same time
which, if the model assumptions hold, should all come from the same normal distribution—see below. To
assess (3), Brown and Forsythe proposed a statistic to formally test homogeneity of variance; see reference
BLANK. The idea is to calculate the absolute deviations of observations from their group medians, and then
perform an ANOVA on the results; if the ANOVA rejects then it is evidence against homogeneous variance7
We can assess the assumption visually with side-by-side boxplots of the absolute deviations which we will
see in the next section.
   As a ﬁnal comment to the reader about checking assumptions, note that the ANOVA process has been
studied by statisticians for decades, under all manner of departures from the ANOVA assumptions, and the
general sense is that ANOVA is quite robust to both nonnormality and heterogeneity of variance. And in a
nightmarish scenario where even ANOVA breaks down, we have a nonparametric alternative; see Section ??.

Example 10.20 (Chicken weights by feed type, checking assumptions). We saw side-by-side boxplots of the
chickwts data in Figure ??. Judging from the plot it looks like the mean of horsebean is less than the rest,
but it is hard to tell what is going on with the other means. In Section ??, we saw how to compute means,
standard deviations, and cell counts for each group; see Example ??. Formal hypothesis tests of normality
can be done with shapiro.test and the by function; see Exercise ??. The tests show that every cell passes
its respective normality test at signiﬁcance level α = 0.05.
   Checking for homogeneity of variance is done via the hovPlot (graph) and hov (test) functions, both
from the HH package. See Exercise ??. We do not detect any problems8. Putting it all together, the chickwts
data seem to be well-suited for a standard analysis of variance.

10.6.1  How to do it with R

Aside from checking assumptions, we ﬁt the ANOVA to the data via the aov function.  It is convenient to
store the model ﬁt as an object and then call anova on the object for the ANOVA table. Note that model is

   7Given that we are still in the middle of the ANOVA section the reader may ﬁnd such reasoning a bit circular. Agreed.
   8But even  if we had found unequal variance, we could do a Welch correction to the denominator degrees of freedom; see
?oneway.test.



218                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


speciﬁed by a model formula that looks like

                               response ∼treatment,

and so typically the ANOVA is performed on a data frame that has response and treatment as quantita-
tive/qualitative column variables, respectively.

> weight.aov <- aov(weight ~ feed, data = chickwts)
> anova(weight.aov)

Analysis of Variance Table

Response: weight
          Df Sum Sq Mean Sq F value    Pr(>F)
feed       5 231129   46226  15.365 5.936e-10 ***
Residuals 65 195556    3009
---
Signif. codes:
0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1

At the top of the ANOVA table the name of the Response variable is identiﬁed, weight in this example.
The ﬁrst column of the table displays the treatment or explanatory variable, feed in this case, followed by
Residuals which stands for the error. The Df column lists the numerator degrees of freedom k −1 (which is
6 −1 = 5 in this example) followed by N −k = 71 −6. The Sum Sq column displays S S T followed by S S E,
then the Mean Sq column is the 3rd column divided by the 2nd (also known as MS T followed by MS E),
and ﬁnally the F ratio MS T/MS E. The p-value is calculated under a null f(df1 = k −1, df2 = N −k),
distribution.
This is a highly signiﬁcant p-value and we consequently reject the null hypothesis that the means are identical.
While we are here, note the following simpler way to calculate the means per group that we did before:

> model.tables(weight.aov, type = "means")

Tables of means
Grand mean

261.3099

 feed
    casein horsebean linseed meatmeal soybean

  We can check that our model is doing a good job by looking at the residuals. Here is a normal qq-plot
of the residuals, shown in Figure 10.4, via the qqPlot function from the RcmdrMisc package. The syntax
could not be simpler; just do qqPlot(weight.aov).

10.7  Sample Size and Power

The power function of a test for a parameter θ is

                                   β(θ) = Pθ(Reject H0), −∞< θ < ∞.

Here are some properties of power functions:



10.7. SAMPLE SIZE AND POWER                                                      219





      2




      1




      0


            −1

            −2      68
                     54

                      −2   −1    0    1    2

                                                            t Quantiles




Figure 10.4: A qq-plot of the residuals from the ANOVA model on the chickwts data. If the model ﬁts well, we would
expect the residuals to be normally distributed (looks pretty good in this case).



220                                      CHAPTER 10. HYPOTHESIS TESTING


   1. β(θ) ≤α for any θ ∈Θ0, and β(θ0) = α. We interpret this by saying that no matter what value θ takes
      inside the null parameter space, there is never more than a chance of α of rejecting the null hypothesis.
    We have controlled the Type I error rate to be no greater than α.

   2. limn→∞β(θ∗) = 1 for any ﬁxed θ∗∈Θ1. In other words, as the sample size grows without bound we
      are able to detect a nonnull value of θ with increasing accuracy, no matter how close it lies to the null
      parameter space. This may appear to be a good thing at ﬁrst glance, but it often turns out to be a curse,
      because this means that our Type II error rate can grow out of control as the sample size increases.

10.7.1  How to do it with R

I am thinking about replicate here, and also power.examp from the TeachingDemos package [188]. See
the excellent plotting functionality in the HH package [90].

> power.examp()

   See Figure 10.5. The plot corresponds to the hypothesis test H0  : µ = µ0 versus H1  : µ = µ1 (where
µ0 = 0 and µ1 = 1, by default) based on a single observation X ∼norm(mean = µ, sd = σ). The top graph is
of the H0 density while the bottom is of the H1 density. The signiﬁcance level is set at α = 0.05, the sample
size is n = 1, and the standard deviation is σ = 1. The pink area is the signiﬁcance level, and the critical
value z0.05 ≈1.645 is marked at the left boundary – this deﬁnes the rejection region. When H0 is true, the
probability of falling in the rejection region is exactly α = 0.05. The same rejection region is marked on
the bottom graph, and the probability of falling in it (when H1 is true) is the blue area shown at the top of
the display to be approximately 0.26. This probability represents the power to detect a non-null mean value
of µ = 1. With the command the run.power.examp() at the command line the same plot opens, but in
addition, there are sliders available that allow the user to interactively change the sample size n, the standard
deviation σ, the true diﬀerence between the means µ1 −µ0, and the signiﬁcance level α. By playing around
the student can investigate the eﬀect each of the aforementioned parameters has on the statistical power. Note
that you need the tkrplot package [?] for run.power.examp.

Chapter 11

Simple Linear Regression




What do I want them to know?

   • basic philosophy of SLR and the regression assumptions

   • point and interval estimation of the model parameters, and how to use it to make predictions

   • point and interval estimation of future observations from the model

   • regression diagnostics, including R2 and basic residual analysis

   • the concept of inﬂuential versus outlying observations, and how to tell the diﬀerence


11.1  Basic Philosophy

Here we have two variables X and Y. For our purposes, X is not random (so we will write x), but Y is random.
We believe that Y depends in some way on x. Some typical examples of (x, Y) pairs are

   • x = study time and Y = score on a test.

   • x = height and Y = weight.

   • x = smoking frequency and Y = age of ﬁrst heart attack.

   Given information about the relationship between x and Y, we would like to predict future values of Y for
particular values of x. This turns out to be a diﬃcult problem1, so instead we ﬁrst tackle an easier problem:
we estimate EY. How can we accomplish this? Well, we know that Y depends somehow on x, so it stands to
reason that
                            EY = µ(x), a function of x.                                  (11.1)

   But we should be able to say more than that. To focus our eﬀorts we impose some structure on the
functional form of µ. For instance,

   •  if µ(x) = β0 + β1x, we try to estimate β0 and β1.

   •  if µ(x) = β0 + β1x + β2x2, we try to estimate β0, β1, and β2.

   •  if µ(x) = β0eβ1x, we try to estimate β0 and β1.

   1Yogi Berra once said, “It is always diﬃcult to make predictions, especially about the future.”


                                          227



228                                 CHAPTER 11. SIMPLE LINEAR REGRESSION


   This helps us in the sense that we concentrate on the estimation of just a few parameters, β0 and β1, say,
rather than some nebulous function. Our modus operandi is simply to perform the random experiment n times
and observe the n ordered pairs of data (x1, Y1), (x2, Y2),  . . . , (xn, Yn). We use these n data points to estimate
the parameters.
   More to the point, there are three simple linear regression (SLR) assumptions that will form the basis for
the rest of this chapter:

Assumption 11.1. We assume that µ is a linear function of x, that is,

                                           µ(x) = β0 + β1x,                                        (11.2)

where β0 and β1 are unknown constants to be estimated.

Assumption 11.2. We further assume that Yi is µ(xi), a “signal”, plus some “error” (represented by the
symbol ϵi):
                                      Yi = β0 + β1xi + ϵi,    i = 1, 2, . . . , n.                              (11.3)
Assumption 11.3. We lastly assume that the errors are IID normal with mean 0 and variance σ2:

                                       ϵ1, ϵ2, . . . , ϵn ∼norm(mean = 0, sd = σ).                           (11.4)

Remark 11.4. We assume both the normality of the errors ϵ and the linearity of the mean function µ. Recall
from Proposition 7.26 of Chapter 7 that if (X, Y) ∼mvnorm then the mean of Y|x is a linear function of x.
This is not a coincidence. In more advanced classes we study the case that both X and Y are random, and in
particular, when they are jointly normally distributed.

11.1.1  What does it all mean?

See Figure 11.1. Shown in the ﬁgure is a solid line, the regression line µ, which in this display has slope 0.5
and y-intercept 2.5, that is, µ(x) = 2.5 + 0.5x. The intuition is that for each given value of x, we observe a
random value of Y which is normally distributed with a mean equal to the height of the regression line at that
x value. Normal densities are superimposed on the plot to drive this point home; in principle, the densities
stand outside of the page, perpendicular to the plane of the paper. The ﬁgure shows three such values of x,
namely, x = 1, x = 2.5, and x = 4. Not only do we assume that the observations at the three locations are
independent, but we also assume that their distributions have the same spread. In mathematical terms this
means that the normal densities all along the line have identical standard deviations – there is no “fanning
out” or “scrunching in” of the normal densities as x increases2.

Example 11.5 (Speed and stopping distance of cars.). We will use the data frame cars from the datasets
package [158]. It has two variables: speed and dist. We can take a look at some of the values in the data
frame:

> head(cars)

  speed dist
1     4    2
2     4   10
3     7    4
4     7   22
5     8   16
6     9   10

   The speed represents how fast the car was going (x) in miles per hour and dist (Y) measures how far it
took the car to stop, in feet. We ﬁrst make a simple scatterplot of the data.
   You can see the output in Figure 11.2, which was produced by the following code.

   2In practical terms, this constant variance assumption is often violated, in that we often observe scatterplots that fan out from the line
as x gets large or small. We say under those circumstances that the data show heteroscedasticity. There are methods to address it, but
they fall outside the realm of SLR.

Figure 11.1: Philosophical foundations of SLR. For each ﬁxed value of x, the distribution of Y is normal. The mean
of the normal distribution follows a straight, linear functional form, and it is the slope and y-intercept of that line which
interests us the most for estimation and hypothesis testing. But also important is that the spread, σ of those normal
distributions are the same for all values of x, so called homogeneity of variance.

   There is a pronounced upward trend to the data points, and the pattern looks approximately linear. There
does not appear to be substantial fanning out of the points or extreme values.

11.2  Estimation

11.2.1  Point Estimates of the Parameters

Where is µ(x)? In essence, we would like to “ﬁt” a line to the points. But how do we determine a “good”
line? Is there a best line? We will use maximum likelihood to ﬁnd it. We know:

                                        Yi = β0 + β1xi + ϵi,    i = 1, . . . , n,                               (11.5)

where the ϵi are IID norm(mean = 0, sd = σ). Thus Yi ∼norm(mean = β0 + β1xi, sd = σ),  i = 1, . . . , n.
Furthermore, Y1, . . . , Yn are independent – but not identically distributed. The likelihood function is:

                                                n
            Y                          L(β0, β1, σ) =      fYi(yi),                                                   (11.6)
                                                 i=1
                                                n              (−(yi −β0 −β1xi)2 )            Y                        =    (2πσ2)−1/2 exp                                   ,                     (11.7)
                                                     2σ2
                                                 i=1
                                 (−Pni=1(yi −β0 −β1xi)2 )                                   =(2πσ2)−n/2 exp                                           .                    (11.8)
                                                    2σ2

We take the natural logarithm to get
                            ln L(β0, β1, σ) = −n ln(2πσ2) − Pni=1(yi −β0 −β1xi)2 .                     (11.9)
                                     2                2σ2

                         ∂           1 Xn                                     ln L = 0 −          2(yi −β0 −β1xi)(−xi),                       (11.12)

for β0 and β1 (in Exercise 46) gives

   The conclusion? To estimate the mean line

                                           µ(x) = β0 + β1x,                                     (11.19)

we use the “line of best ﬁt”
                                                      ˆµ(x) = ˆβ0 + ˆβ1x,                                     (11.20)
where ˆβ0 and ˆβ1 are given as above.  For notation we will usually write b0 =  ˆβ0 and b1 =  ˆβ1 so that
ˆµ(x) = b0 + b1x.
Remark 11.6. The formula for b1 in Equation (11.17) gets the job done but does not really make any sense.
There are many equivalent formulas for b1 that are more intuitive, or at the least are easier to remember. One
of the author’s favorites is
                                                                sy
                                               b1 = r    ,                                        (11.21)
                                                             sx
where r, sy, and sx are the sample correlation coeﬃcient and the sample standard deviations of the Y and x
data, respectively. See Exercise 47. Also, notice the similarity between Equation (11.21) and Equation (7.35).

How to do it with R

Here we go. R will calculate the linear regression line with the lm function. We will store the result in an
object which we will call cars.lm. Here is how it works:

> cars.lm <- lm(dist ~ speed, data = cars)

   The ﬁrst part of the input to the lm function, dist   speed, is a model formula, read like this: dist is
described (or modeled) by speed. The data = cars argument tells R where to look for the variables quoted
in the model formula. The output object cars.lm contains a multitude of information. Let’s ﬁrst take a look
at the coeﬃcients of the ﬁtted regression line, which are extracted by the coef function (alternatively, we
could just type cars.lm to see the same thing):

> coef(cars.lm)

(Intercept)       speed
 -17.579095    3.932409

   The parameter estimates b0 and b1 for the intercept and slope, respectively, are shown above.
     It is good practice to visually inspect the data with the regression line added to the plot. To do this we
ﬁrst scatterplot the original data and then follow with a call to the abline function. The inputs to abline
are the coeﬃcients of cars.lm; see Figure 11.3.
   To calculate points on the regression line we may simply plug the desired x value(s) into ˆµ, either by
hand, or with the predict function. The inputs to predict are the ﬁtted linear model object, cars.lm, and
the desired x value(s) represented by a data frame. See the example below.

Example 11.7. Using the regression line for the cars data:

   1. What is the meaning of µ(8) = β0 + β1(8)? This represents the average stopping distance (in feet) for a
      car going 8 mph.



11.2. ESTIMATION                                                                   233


   2. Interpret the slope β1. The true slope β1 represents the increase in average stopping distance for each
      mile per hour faster that the car drives. In this case, we estimate the car to take approximately 3.93
      additional feet to stop for each additional mph increase in speed.

   3. Interpret the intercept β0. This would represent the mean stopping distance for a car traveling 0 mph
     (which our regression line estimates to be −17.58. Of course, this interpretation does not make any
      sense for this example, because a car travelling 0 mph takes 0 ft to stop (it was not moving in the ﬁrst
      place)! What went wrong? Looking at the data, we notice that the smallest speed for which we have
     measured data is 4 mph. Therefore, if we predict what would happen for slower speeds then we would
     be extrapolating, a dangerous practice which often gives nonsensical results.

11.2.2  Point Estimates of the Regression Line
We said at the beginning of the chapter that our goal was to estimate µ = EY, and the arguments in Sec-
tion 11.2.1 showed how to obtain an estimate ˆµ of µ when the regression assumptions hold. Now we will
reap the beneﬁts of our work in more ways than we previously disclosed. Given a particular value x0, there
are two values we would like to estimate:

   1. the mean value of Y at x0, and

   2. a future value of Y at x0. The ﬁrst is a number, µ(x0), and the second is a random variable, Y(x0), but
      our point estimate is the same for both: ˆµ(x0).

Example 11.8. We may use the regression line to obtain a point estimate of the mean stopping distance for a
car traveling 8 mph: ˆµ(15) = b0 + (8)(b1) which is approximately 13.88. We would also use 13.88 as a point
estimate for the stopping distance of a future car traveling 8 mph.

   Note that we actually have observed data for a car traveling 8 mph; its stopping distance was 16 ft as
listed in the ﬁfth row of the cars data which we saw in Example 11.5.

> cars[5, ]

  speed dist
5     8   16

   There is a special name for estimates ˆµ(x0) when x0 matches an observed value xi from the data set. They
are called ﬁtted values, they are denoted by ˆY1, ˆY2, ..., ˆYn (ignoring repetition), and they play an important
role in the sections that follow.
    In an abuse of notation we will sometimes write ˆY or ˆY(x0) to denote a point on the regression line even
when x0 does not belong to the original data if the context of the statement obviates any danger of confusion.
  We saw in Example 11.7 that spooky things can happen when we are cavalier about point estimation.
While it is usually acceptable to predict/estimate at values of x0 that fall within the range of the original x
data, it is reckless to use ˆµ for point estimates at locations outside that range. Such estimates are usually
worthless. Do not extrapolate unless there are compelling external reasons, and even then, temper it with a
good deal of caution.


How to do it with R

The ﬁtted values are automatically computed as a byproduct of the model ﬁtting procedure and are already
stored as a component of the cars.lm object. We may access them with the fitted function (we only show
the ﬁrst ﬁve entries):

> fitted(cars.lm)[1:5]

        1         2         3         4         5
-1.849460 -1.849460  9.947766  9.947766 13.880175



234                                 CHAPTER 11. SIMPLE LINEAR REGRESSION


    Predictions at x values that are not necessarily part of the original data are done with the predict func-
tion. The ﬁrst argument is the original cars.lm object and the second argument newdata accepts a dataframe
(in the same form that was used to ﬁt cars.lm) that contains the locations at which we are seeking predic-
tions. Let us predict the average stopping distances of cars traveling 6 mph, 8 mph, and 21 mph:

> predict(cars.lm, newdata = data.frame(speed = c(6, 8, 21)))

        1         2         3
 6.015358 13.880175 65.001489

   Note that there were no observed cars that traveled 6 mph or 21 mph. Also note that our estimate for a
car traveling 8 mph matches the value we computed by hand in Example 11.8.

11.2.3  Mean Square Error and Standard Error

To ﬁnd the MLE of σ2 we consider the partial derivative
                         ∂        n      1 Xn                                     ln L =    −              (yi −β0 −β1xi)2,                       (11.22)
                        ∂σ2      2σ2   2(σ2)2
                                                                      i=1

and after plugging in ˆβ0 and ˆβ1 and setting equal to zero we get

                                             n                           n
                             1 X                1 X                                   ˆσ2 =        (yi −ˆβ0 −ˆβ1xi)2 =        [yi −ˆµ(xi)]2.                      (11.23)
                             n                   n
                                              i=1                           i=1

We write ˆYi = ˆµ(xi), and we let Ei = Yi −ˆYi be the ith residual. We see

                                          n
                       nˆσ2 = X E2i = S S E = the sum of squared errors.                     (11.24)
                                            i=1

For a point estimate of σ2 we use the mean square error S 2 deﬁned by

                                                         2   S S E                                       S =                                              (11.25)                                           n −2,
                           √
and we estimate σ with the standard error S =  S 23.


How to do it with R

The residuals for the model may be obtained with the residuals function; we only show the ﬁrst few entries
in the interest of space:

> residuals(cars.lm)[1:5]

        1         2         3         4         5
 3.849460 11.849460 -5.947766 12.052234  2.119825

    In the last section, we calculated the ﬁtted value for x = 8 and found it to be approximately ˆµ(8) ≈
13.88. Now, it turns out that there was only one recorded observation at x = 8, and we have seen this
value in the output of head(cars) in Example 11.5; it was dist = 16 ft for a car with speed = 8 mph.
Therefore, the residual should be E = Y −ˆY which is E ≈16−13.88. Now take a look at the last entry of
residuals(cars.lm), above. It is not a coincidence.
   The estimate S for σ is called the Residual standard error and for the cars data is shown a few
lines up on the summary(cars.lm) output (see How to do it with R in Section 11.2.4). We may read it from
there to be S ≈15.38, or we can access it directly from the summary object.

   3Be careful not to confuse the mean square error S 2 with the sample variance S 2 in Chapter 3. Other notation the reader may
encounter is the lowercase s2 or the bulky MS E.

11.2.4  Interval Estimates of the Parameters

We discussed general interval estimation in Chapter 9. There we found that we could use what we know
about the sampling distribution of certain statistics to construct conﬁdence intervals for the parameter being
estimated. We will continue in that vein, and to get started we will determine the sampling distributions of
the parameter estimates, b1 and b0.
   To that end, we can see from Equation (11.17) (and it is made clear in Chapter 12) that b1 is just a linear
combination of normally distributed random variables, so b1 is normally distributed too. Further, it can be
shown that
                                   b1 ∼norm mean = β1, sd = σb1                              (11.26)

where
                                 σ
                                        σb1 =                                                  (11.27)
                                pPni=1(xi −x)2
is called the standard error of b1 which unfortunately depends on the unknown value of σ. We do not lose
heart, though, because we can estimate σ with the standard error S from the last section. This gives us an
estimate S b1 for σb1 deﬁned by
                                             S
                                  S b1 =                           .                                  (11.28)
                                pPni=1(xi −x)2
   Now, it turns out that b0, b1, and S are mutually independent (see the footnote in Section 12.2.4). There-
fore, the quantity
                                                  b1 −β1
                                   T =                                               (11.29)
                                            S b1
has a t(df = n −2) distribution and a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for β1 is given by

                                       b1 ± tα/2(df = n −1) S b1.                                 (11.30)

     It is also sometimes of interest to construct a conﬁdence interval for β0 in which case we will need the
sampling distribution of b0. It is shown in Chapter 12 that

                                   b0 ∼norm mean = β0, sd = σb0  ,                            (11.31)

where σb0 is given by
                     s                                        1         x2
                                     σb0 = σ   +                       ,                               (11.32)
                                        n  Pni=1(xi −x)2

and which we estimate with the S b0 deﬁned by

                     s                                        1         x2
                                S b0 = S   +                       .                               (11.33)
                                        n  Pni=1(xi −x)2

Thus the quantity
                                                  b0 −β0
                                   T =                                               (11.34)
                                            S b0
has a t(df = n −2) distribution and a 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval for β0 is given by

                                       b0 ± tα/2(df = n −1) S b0.                                 (11.35)

conﬁdence interval for β1 we could use b1 = 3.932 and S b1 = 0.416 together with a t0.025(df = 23) critical
value to calculate b1 ± t0.025(df = 23) · S b1. Or, we could use the confint function.

11.2.5  Interval Estimates of the Regression Line

We have seen how to estimate the coeﬃcients of regression line with both point estimates and conﬁdence
intervals. We even saw how to estimate a value ˆµ(x) on the regression line for a given value of x, such as
x = 15.
   But how good is our estimate ˆµ(15)? How much conﬁdence do we have in this estimate? Furthermore,
suppose we were going to observe another value of Y at x = 15. What could we say?
    Intuitively, it should be easier to get bounds on the mean (average) value of Y at x0 – called a conﬁdence
interval for the mean value of Y at x0 – than it is to get bounds on a future observation of Y (called a prediction
interval for Y at x0). As we shall see, the intuition serves us well and conﬁdence intervals are shorter for the
mean value, longer for the individual value.
   Our point estimate of µ(x0) is of course ˆY = ˆY(x0), so for a conﬁdence interval we will need to know ˆY’s
sampling distribution. It turns out (see Section ) that ˆY = ˆµ(x0) is distributed

                           s                                                  1      (x0 −x)2
                                     ˆY ∼norm     = µ(x0), sd = σ   +                                 (11.36)                                                                      mean                                                  n  Pni=1(xi −x)2  .



11.2. ESTIMATION                                                                   237


   Since σ is unknown we estimate it with S (we should expect the appearance of a t(df = n−2) distribution
in the near future).
  A 100(1 −α)% conﬁdence interval (CI) for µ(x0) is given by

                        s                                              1      (x0 −x2)
                                              ˆY ± tα/2(df = n −2) S   +                       .                        (11.37)
                                              n  Pni=1(xi −x)2

Prediction intervals are a little bit diﬀerent. In order to ﬁnd conﬁdence bounds for a new observation of Y
(we will denote it Ynew) we use the fact that

                           s                                                     1      (x0 −x)2
                Ynew ∼norm     = µ(x0), sd = σ  1 +  +                              (11.38)                                                     n  Pni=1(xi −x)2  .                                                                      mean

Of course, σ is unknown so we estimate it with S and a 100(1−α)% prediction interval (PI) for a future value
of Y at x0 is given by
                         s                                                 1      (x0 −x)2
                               ˆY(x0) ± tα/2(df = n −1) S   1 +  +                       .                    (11.39)
                                                 n  Pni=1(xi −x)2

We notice that the prediction interval in Equation (11.39) is wider than the conﬁdence interval in Equation
(11.37), as we expected at the beginning of the section.


How to do it with R

Conﬁdence and prediction intervals are calculated in R with the predict function, which we encountered
in Section 11.2.2. There we neglected to take advantage of its additional interval argument. The general
syntax follows.

Example 11.9. We will ﬁnd conﬁdence and prediction intervals for the stopping distance of a car travelling
5, 6, and 21 mph (note from the graph that there are no collected data for these speeds). We have computed
cars.lm earlier, and we will use this for input to the predict function. Also, we need to tell R the values of
x0 at which we want the predictions made, and store the x0 values in a data frame whose variable is labeled
with the correct name. This is important.

> new <- data.frame(speed = c(5, 6, 21))

   Next we instruct R to calculate the intervals. Conﬁdence intervals are given by

> predict(cars.lm, newdata = new, interval = "confidence")

        fit       lwr      upr
1  2.082949 -7.644150 11.81005
2  6.015358 -2.973341 15.00406
3 65.001489 58.597384 71.40559

    Prediction intervals are given by

> predict(cars.lm, newdata = new, interval = "prediction")

        fit       lwr      upr
1  2.082949 -30.33359 34.49948
2  6.015358 -26.18731 38.21803
3 65.001489  33.42257 96.58040

   The type of interval is dictated by the interval argument (which is none by default), and the default
conﬁdence level is 95% (which can be changed with the level argument).



238                                 CHAPTER 11. SIMPLE LINEAR REGRESSION


Example 11.10. Using the cars data,

   1. Report a point estimate of and a 95% conﬁdence interval for the mean stopping distance for a car
       travelling 5 mph. The ﬁtted value for x = 5 is 2.08, so a point estimate would be 2.08 ft. The 95% CI is
      given by -7.64 to 11.81, so with 95% conﬁdence the mean stopping distance lies somewhere between
      -7.64 ft and 11.81 ft.

   2. Report a point prediction for and a 95% prediction interval for the stopping distance of a hypothetical
      car travelling 21 mph. The ﬁtted value for x = 21 is 65, so a point prediction for the stopping distance
       is 65 ft. The 95% PI is 33.42 to 96.58, so with 95% conﬁdence we may assert that the hypothetical
      stopping distance for a car travelling 21 mph would lie somewhere between 33.42 ft and 96.58 ft.

11.2.6  Graphing the Conﬁdence and Prediction Bands

We earlier guessed that a bound on the value of a single new observation would be inherently less certain
than a bound for an average (mean) value; therefore, we expect the CIs for the mean to be tighter than the PIs
for a new observation. A close look at the standard deviations in Equations (11.37) and (11.39) conﬁrms our
guess, but we would like to see a picture to drive the point home.
  We may plot the conﬁdence and prediction intervals with one fell swoop using the ci.plot function
from the HH package [90]. The graph is displayed in Figure 11.4.

> library(HH)
> ci.plot(cars.lm)

   Notice that the bands curve outward from the regression line as the x values move away from the center.
This is expected once we notice the (x0 −x)2 term in the standard deviation formulas in Equations (11.37)
and (11.39).

11.3  Model Utility and Inference

11.3.1  Hypothesis Tests for the Parameters
Much of the attention of SLR is directed toward β1 because when β1 , 0 the mean value of Y increases
(or decreases) as x increases.  It is really boring when β1 = 0, because in that case the mean value of Y
remains the same, regardless of the value of x (when the regression assumptions hold, of course). It is thus
very important to decide whether or not β1 = 0. We address the question with a statistical test of the null
hypothesis H0  : β1 = 0 versus the alternative hypothesis H1  : β1 , 0, and to do that we need to know the
sampling distribution of b1 when the null hypothesis is true.
   To this end we already know from Section 11.2.4 that the quantity

                                                  b1 −β1
                                   T =                                               (11.40)
                                            S b1
has a t(df = n −2) distribution; therefore, when β1 = 0 the quantity b1/S b1 has a t(df = n −2) distribution
and we can compute a p-value by comparing the observed value of b1/S b1 with values under a t(df = n −2)
curve.
    Similarly, we may test the hypothesis H0  : β0 = 0 versus the alternative H1  : β0 , 0 with the statistic
T = b0/S b0, where S b0 is given in Section 11.2.4. The test is conducted the same way as for β1.


How to do it with R

Let us take another look at the output from summary(cars.lm):

> summary(cars.lm)
